The resulting convolution identity may therefore be divided by the first factor’s series, whose constant coefficient is one. This is Equation <a href="#eq:cut-generating-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cut-generating-ratio">[eq:cut-generating-ratio]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Ordinary cohomology and Hausel’s formula

Let $\nu_{\det}=\prod_i\det_{V_i}$ denote determinant stability, and write $X_{\det}(v,w)$ for the corresponding quiver variety. This is a regular choice: its pairing with every nonzero nonnegative dimension vector is strictly positive. Here is the corresponding GIT criterion, including freeness. Write $I_i:W_i\to V_i$ for the incoming framing maps. In the convention that semi-invariants satisfy $f(gx)=\nu_{\det}(g)^a f(x)$, semistability is cyclicity: the smallest collection of subspaces invariant under all doubled-quiver arrows and containing every $\mathop{\mathrm{im}}I_i$ is $V$. If that collection is proper, a one-parameter subgroup with weight zero on it and weight $-1$ on a chosen complement has a limit on the point and negative determinant weight. Every semi-invariant of positive determinant weight must therefore vanish. Conversely, at a cyclic point choose a basis at each vertex from images of framing vectors under arrow paths. The product of their basis determinants is a nonzero semi-invariant of weight $\nu_{\det}$. On the principal open where this product is nonzero, normalizing these path bases gives a slice meeting each orbit once. A semistable orbit limit is cyclic, and one of its such principal opens also meets the original orbit; the slice then shows that the limit belongs to that orbit. Thus these orbits are closed in the semistable locus. Moreover, a stabilizer fixes all path images of framing vectors, and hence is the identity. With the opposite linearization convention the dual argument gives cocyclicity. The argument applies to the closed moment fiber and allows loops throughout. Put $$P_{X_{\det}(v,w)}(q)
 =\sum_{j\geq0}\dim H^{2j}(X_{\det}(v,w);\mathbb C)q^j.$$ Odd cohomology vanishes by Kirwan surjectivity, so this is the full ordinary cohomology polynomial in half degrees. The empty variety has polynomial zero.

<a id="stmt-prop-hausel-identification"></a>
<div id="prop:hausel-identification" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:hausel-cut-bound"></a>
**Proposition 18**. *For a quiver with a loop at every vertex of positive dimension, $$\label{eq:hausel-cut-bound}
 P_{X_{\det}(v,w)}(q)
 =\sum_{\|m\|=v}
   q^{C(v)-D_m+w\cdot(v-l)}h_m(q).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-hausel-identification"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-hausel-identification</summary>

*Proof.* We first specify the moment-level comparison. Give $N$ a Hermitian metric invariant under $G_c=\prod_iU(V_i)$, and equip $T^*N$ with its flat hyperkähler structure. The hyperkähler moment map has one real and one complex component. Under the trace identification of the central real dual with $\mathbb R^{Q_0}$, let $\eta=(1,\ldots,1)$ be the level corresponding to the determinant character. The GIT–moment-map correspondence identifies $X_{\det}(v,w)$ with the compact-group quotient at real level $\eta$ and complex level zero. Rotation of the three moment-map components carries this quotient to the one at real level zero and complex level $c\eta$, for a common nonzero normalization constant $c$. At real level zero, affine Kempf–Ness identifies the latter quotient with $$\mu_{\mathbb C}^{-1}(c\eta)/\!/G.$$ These are identifications of underlying topological spaces; they do not assert that rotation is holomorphic in the original complex structure. The comparison uses the direct rotation on the flat representation (Hitchin et al. 1987, sec. 3(D)) <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[HKLR87](#ref-HKLR87)] and the topological affine Kempf–Ness theorem recalled in Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>. The quiver GIT description is explained in (Nakajima 1994) <a id="pm-cite-55"></a>[[Nakajima94](#ref-Nakajima94)]; the flat-representation argument just given includes loops. Multiplying the cotangent coordinate by $c^{-1}$ is an algebraic isomorphism from this affine quotient to $$M_\eta(v,w)=\mu_{\mathbb C}^{-1}
       ((\mathrm{id}_{V_i})_i)/\!/G,$$ because the cotangent moment map is linear in that coordinate. Thus $M_\eta(v,w)$ and $X_{\det}(v,w)$ have the same ordinary cohomology, including simultaneous emptiness.

<a id="eq:hausel-ordinary"></a>
Hausel’s formula for $M_\eta(v,w)$ explicitly allows loops and multiple arrows (Hausel 2006, Equation (9)) <a id="pm-cite-56"></a>[[Hausel06](#ref-Hausel06)]. We record its grading and all factors needed here. For partitions $\lambda^i$ and $\lambda^j$, write $$\langle\lambda^i,\lambda^j\rangle
 =\sum_{a,b}\min(\lambda_a^i,\lambda_b^j),\qquad
 E_Q(\lambda)
 =\sum_{a:i\to j\in Q_1}\langle\lambda^i,\lambda^j\rangle
   -\sum_i\langle\lambda^i,\lambda^i\rangle.$$ Let $m(\lambda)_{i,s}$ be the number of parts of size $s$ in $\lambda^i$, and set $$\delta(v,w)=\sum_{a:i\to j\in Q_1}v_iv_j
                  -\sum_i v_i^2+w\cdot v.$$ The nonempty smooth variety has complex dimension $2\delta(v,w)$. With $q=t^2$, where $t$ is the ordinary cohomological variable in Hausel’s formula, that formula reads $$\label{eq:hausel-ordinary}
 \sum_v q^{-\delta(v,w)}P_{M_\eta(v,w)}(q)z^v
 =
 \frac{\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}
       z^{|\lambda|}
       q^{-E_Q(\lambda)-\sum_i\langle\lambda^i,(1^{w_i})\rangle}
       /(q)_{m(\lambda)}}
      {\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}
       z^{|\lambda|}q^{-E_Q(\lambda)}/(q)_{m(\lambda)}}.$$ Here $\lambda$ ranges over all multipartitions, including the empty one. In particular, the left side uses ordinary cohomology. Its normalization can also be checked by Poincaré duality: if $u$ is the arithmetic variable, a smooth variety of complex dimension $2\delta$ satisfies $P_c(u)=u^{2\delta}P(u^{-1})$. The normalized count $u^{-\delta}P_c(u)$ therefore becomes $q^{-\delta}P(q)$ after setting $u=q^{-1}$, as in Equation <a href="#eq:hausel-ordinary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hausel-ordinary">[eq:hausel-ordinary]</a>.

Now write $m=m(\lambda)$. Removing one loop at each vertex cancels the vertex self-pairing in $E_Q$, and therefore $$E_Q(\lambda)=D_m,
 \qquad
 \sum_i\langle\lambda^i,(1^{w_i})\rangle
 =\sum_i w_i l_i=w(m),
 \qquad
 \delta(v,w)=C(v)+w\cdot v.$$ Consequently the right side of Equation <a href="#eq:hausel-ordinary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hausel-ordinary">[eq:hausel-ordinary]</a> is exactly the ratio in Equation <a href="#eq:cut-generating-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cut-generating-ratio">[eq:cut-generating-ratio]</a>. Taking its $z^v$ coefficient and multiplying by $q^{C(v)+w\cdot v}$ proves Equation <a href="#eq:hausel-cut-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hausel-cut-bound">[eq:hausel-cut-bound]</a>.

The right side is a finite polynomial before this identification: Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-cut-quotient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-cut-quotient">15</a> makes each $h_m$ finite, there are only finitely many multipartitions of $v$, and the shifts are nonnegative integers. Explicitly, $$C(v)-D_m
 =\tfrac12\sum_{i,j,s,t}c_{ij}
          \bigl(st-\min(s,t)\bigr)m_{i,s}m_{j,t}\geq0,
 \qquad
 w\cdot(v-l)=\sum_{i,s}w_i(s-1)m_{i,s}\geq0.$$ The diagonal entries $c_{ii}$ are even and the off-diagonal terms pair, which also verifies integrality. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-counting-empty-cases"></a>
<div id="rem:counting-empty-cases" class="remark">

**Remark 19**. When $v=0$, only the empty multipartition occurs and the polynomial is one. When $w=0$ and $v\ne0$, the cut consisting of all positive slots has $q_S=1$, so every summand is zero. Equivalently, the numerator and denominator of Equation <a href="#eq:cut-generating-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cut-generating-ratio">[eq:cut-generating-ratio]</a> coincide. This agrees with emptiness at determinant stability: the diagonal gauge scalar acts trivially on the fields and nontrivially on the linearization. An unframed connected component of the supported graph gives the same unit cut. Thus neither the recurrence nor the Betti comparison omits these degenerate cases.

</div>

<a id="stmt-cor-loop-isomorphism"></a>
<div id="cor:loop-isomorphism" class="corollary">

**Corollary 20**. *Suppose that the quiver has a loop at every vertex of positive dimension. At determinant stability, the coefficient map induces a canonical isomorphism of graded $R_0$-algebras $$B_0\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }H^*(X_{\det}(v,w);\mathbb C).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-loop-isomorphism"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-loop-isomorphism</summary>

*Proof.* Proposition <a href="#prop:kernel-inclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:kernel-inclusion">8</a> gives the displayed graded $R_0$-algebra surjection. Proposition <a href="#prop:filtration-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:filtration-bound">13</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:hausel-identification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:hausel-identification">18</a> bound the dimension of each graded piece of its source from above by that of its target. Surjectivity gives the reverse inequality. Its kernel therefore vanishes in every degree. Since the bijection is the original coefficient-compatible algebra map, this is an isomorphism over $R_0$ and an equality of the two coefficient ideals, with the quotient rings retaining nilpotents. The same argument includes the zero ring in the empty case. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:deformations"></a>
### Flavor parameters, loop removal, and stability

Throughout this section, $B_F$ denotes the quotient by the full topological torus. In particular, it is independent of the stability character. We first prove that regular stabilities have the same Betti numbers. We then lift a no-flavor comparison to arbitrary flavor parameters and use an added-loop parameter to remove the loop hypothesis at determinant stability. These three inputs combine in Theorem <a href="#thm:full-torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:full-torus">24</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-betti-independence"></a>
<div id="lem:betti-independence" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21**. *Fix a quiver and dimension and framing vectors. The ordinary Betti numbers of $X_\theta=\mu^{-1}(0)/\!/_{\theta}G$ are independent of the regular character $\theta$, where regular means that every semistable point is stable and the action on the semistable locus is free. Empty quotients are included.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-betti-independence"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-betti-independence</summary>

*Proof.* By Lemma <a href="#lem:local-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-stability">5</a>, each regular character has a real open ball in which every rational parameter gives the same stable and semistable loci, geometric quotient, and principal gauge bundle. We may therefore perturb within this ball without changing the quotient.

<a id="eq:rotation-affine-fiber"></a>
Here is a comparison of such neighborhoods for different regular characters. Write $G_c=\prod_i U(V_i)$ for a compact form and $\mathfrak c=(\mathfrak g^*)^G$ for the space of complex central moment parameters. Equip $T^*N$ with its standard flat hyperkähler structure. The character space identifies with a real form of $\mathfrak c$. For a rational parameter, clear its denominators and then dilate the flat representation by the inverse square root of that common denominator; the quadratic moment maps thereby return to level $\theta$. Kempf–Ness identifies $X_\theta$ with the quotient by $G_c$ at real moment level $\theta$ and complex moment level zero. Quaternionic rotation commutes with $G_c$ and rotates the three moment maps. Thus it identifies this space with the quotient at real level zero and complex level $c\theta$, where $c\ne0$ is one fixed scalar determined by the normalization of the moment maps. Applying affine Kempf–Ness at this latter level gives a homeomorphism $$\label{eq:rotation-affine-fiber}
 X_\theta\ \simeq\ \mu^{-1}(c\theta)/\!/G .$$ This uses the flat hyperkähler quotient construction (Hitchin et al. 1987, sec. 3(D)) <a id="pm-cite-57"></a>[[HKLR87](#ref-HKLR87)] and the topological affine Kempf–Ness identification recalled in Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a>; the quiver-variety description appears in (Nakajima 1994) <a id="pm-cite-58"></a>[[Nakajima94](#ref-Nakajima94)]. The rotation here acts directly on the flat representation and is valid with loops. It requires no properness of a moment-map family. One can normalize $c=1$; alternatively a linear dilation of $T^*N$ identifies any two common nonzero scalar multiples of the complex level.

Consider the affine family $$\pi:\mathcal Z=
 \mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathbb C[\{(x,\zeta)\in T^*N\times\mathfrak c:
                       \mu(x)=\zeta\}]^G
 \longrightarrow\mathfrak c.$$ Its fiber at $\zeta$ is $\mu^{-1}(\zeta)/\!/G$. To check this without a flatness assumption, let $A$ be the coordinate ring inside the brackets. The ideal specifying $\zeta$ is generated by invariant parameter functions. Exactness of invariants and the Reynolds operator give $$(A/\mathfrak m_\zeta A)^G
       =A^G/\mathfrak m_\zeta A^G.$$ The morphism $\pi$ is of finite type. Constructibility of compactly supported direct image and its fiber-stalk description (Maxim and Schürmann 2021, Theorem 2.5(b)) <a id="pm-cite-59"></a>[[MaximSchurmann21](#ref-MaximSchurmann21)] <a id="pm-cite-60"></a>[[SchnurerSoergel16](#ref-SchnurerSoergel16)] (Schnürer and Soergel 2016, Theorem 5.10) therefore imply that $R\pi_!\mathbb C_{\mathcal Z}$ has constructible cohomology sheaves with stalks $H_c^*(\pi^{-1}(\zeta);\mathbb C)$. On a common dense Zariski open subset $\Omega\subset\mathfrak c$, their ranks, hence all compactly supported Betti numbers of the fibers, are constant. We may take $\Omega$ connected. Every real open ball in the character space is Zariski dense after multiplication by $c$, and its rational points are dense in the usual topology. Each regular character can thus be perturbed, without changing its quotient, so that $c\theta\in\Omega$.

By Equation <a href="#eq:rotation-affine-fiber" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rotation-affine-fiber">[eq:rotation-affine-fiber]</a>, all regular quotients have the same compactly supported Betti numbers. A nonempty such quotient is smooth of complex dimension $2(\dim N-\dim G)$: freeness makes the moment map a submersion along its stable locus. Poincaré duality now gives equality of ordinary Betti numbers. If one regular quotient is empty, all compactly supported Betti numbers vanish for every regular quotient; a nonempty smooth quotient would have nonzero top compactly supported cohomology, a contradiction. This also covers nonfaithful gauge actions and unframed components: the generic central fiber is allowed to be empty. If $G$ is trivial, the parameter space is a point and the assertion is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-flavor-lift"></a>
<div id="lem:flavor-lift" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22**. *For a fixed regular stability, suppose that the coefficient map induces $B_0\simeq H^*(X)$. Then, for every commuting flavor torus $F$, the map of Proposition <a href="#prop:kernel-inclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:kernel-inclusion">8</a> is an isomorphism $$B_F\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }H_F^*(X)$$ of graded $R$-algebras.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-flavor-lift"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-flavor-lift</summary>

*Proof.* Set $S=\mathbb C[\mathfrak f]$ and let $S_+$ be its positive-degree ideal. Kirwan surjectivity implies $H^{\mathrm{odd}}(X)=0$. The Borel spectral sequence for $X\to EF\times_F X\to BF$ has second page $H^*(BF)\otimes H^*(X)$, concentrated in even total degrees. It degenerates, and its edge map to $H^*(X)$ is surjective. Lifting a finite homogeneous basis of $H^*(X)$ and applying Leray–Hirsch shows that $H_F^*(X)$ is a free $S$-module with $$H_F^*(X)/S_+H_F^*(X)=H^*(X).$$ This remains valid for $X=\varnothing$, with zero modules.

Let $J$ be the kernel of the surjection $B_F\twoheadrightarrow H_F^*(X)$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:kernel-inclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:kernel-inclusion">8</a>. Freeness of the target makes $\operatorname{Tor}_1^S(H_F^*(X),S/S_+)$ vanish. Tensoring its defining exact sequence with $S/S_+$ consequently gives $$0\longrightarrow J/S_+J\longrightarrow B_F/S_+B_F
       \longrightarrow H^*(X)\longrightarrow0.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:flavor-base-change" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:flavor-base-change">4</a> identifies the middle term and map with $B_0\xrightarrow{\sim}H^*(X)$; hence $J=S_+J$. The module $J$ is graded and bounded below, since $B_F$ is a quotient of the nonnegatively graded ring $R$. If $J$ had a nonzero homogeneous element of least degree, the identity $J=S_+J$ would express it using elements of smaller degree, which is impossible. Thus $J=0$. This argument uses positivity only for the coefficient quotient $B_F$, and does not assume that the Coulomb algebra itself is nonnegatively graded. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-loop-removal"></a>
<div id="prop:loop-removal" class="proposition">

**Proposition 23**. *For every finite quiver and every dimension and framing vector, at the determinant stability used in Corollary <a href="#cor:loop-isomorphism" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:loop-isomorphism">20</a>, the coefficient map induces an isomorphism of graded $R_0$-algebras $$B_0\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }H^*(X).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-loop-removal"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-loop-removal</summary>

<a id="eq:enlarged-flavor-isomorphism"></a>
<a id="eq:loop-local-ring"></a>
*Proof.* Discard zero-rank gauge factors. Add one loop at each remaining vertex and write $$N^+=N\oplus\bigoplus_i\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(V_i).$$ Let $H=\mathbb C^\times$ act trivially on $N$ and with weight one on every added loop. Put $H^*_H(\mathrm{pt})=\mathbb C[z]$, with $\deg z=2$, and use a superscript $+$ for the enlarged theory. At determinant stability, Corollary <a href="#cor:loop-isomorphism" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:loop-isomorphism">20</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:flavor-lift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:flavor-lift">22</a> give $$\label{eq:enlarged-flavor-isomorphism}
 B_H^+\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }H_H^*(X^+)
 \qquad\text{as graded }R_0[z]\text{-algebras}.$$ We compare this with the original theory endowed with the trivial $H$-action. Let $\mathfrak m_0\subset R_0$ be the maximal ideal of the graded origin and set $$\label{eq:loop-local-ring}
 \Lambda=R_0[z]_{(\mathfrak m_0,z-1)}.$$ The comparison over $\Lambda$ is ungraded in cohomological degree but retains topological charge. The equality of homogeneous ideals in $R_0$ obtained below will restore the cohomological grading.

<a id="eq:loop-coulomb-localization"></a>
*The Coulomb comparison.* Use the common rational Laurent realization of Proposition <a href="#prop:minuscule" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:minuscule">3</a>, and put $P=\mathbb C[x_{ia}]$. For a positive or negative minuscule coweight $\lambda$, the added numerator in Equation <a href="#eq:minuscule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:minuscule">[eq:minuscule]</a> is $$E_\lambda(x,z)=
   \prod_i\prod_{a,b=1}^{v_i}
     (z+x_{ib}-x_{ia})^{(\lambda_{ia}-\lambda_{ib})_+}.$$ It is invariant under the stabilizer $\mathcal W_\lambda$ and has value one at $(x,z)=(0,1)$. Its inverse belongs to $P[z]^{\mathcal W_\lambda}\otimes_{R_0[z]}\Lambda$: indeed, $$\Delta_\lambda=\prod_{w\in\mathcal W}wE_\lambda
       \in R_0[z],\qquad \Delta_\lambda(0,1)=1,$$ and $E_\lambda^{-1}=(\Delta_\lambda/E_\lambda)/\Delta_\lambda$. If $M(\lambda,p)$ denotes an original dressed generator, the enlarged generator with dressing $p$ is $M(\lambda,pE_\lambda)$. Conversely, dressing by $p/E_\lambda$ recovers $M(\lambda,p)$ after localization. Generation therefore identifies the two localized Coulomb algebras, with their coefficient maps and their full topological gradings. The elements inverted in Equation <a href="#eq:loop-local-ring" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:loop-local-ring">[eq:loop-local-ring]</a> have topological degree zero. Thus localization commutes with quotienting by all nonzero topological degrees, and gives $$\label{eq:loop-coulomb-localization}
 B_H^+\otimes_{R_0[z]}\Lambda
    \simeq (B_0\otimes_\mathbb C\mathbb C[z])\otimes_{R_0[z]}\Lambda.$$

*The cohomological comparison.* Let $D^+\subset T^*N^+$ be the stable zero moment locus. An $H$-fixed point of the quotient may be fixed only after a compensating gauge transformation. We therefore localize the actual $H$-fixed locus of this prequotient, retaining $G\times H$ equivariance, and first restrict to $T\times H$. Brion’s subgroup localization theorem (Brion 1998, Lemma 4) <a id="pm-cite-61"></a>[[Brion98](#ref-Brion98)] says that, for a torus space embedded equivariantly in a finite-dimensional representation, restriction to the fixed locus of a subtorus becomes an isomorphism after inverting finitely many characters nontrivial on that subtorus. Apply it to the compact forms of $T\times H$ and $H$ and to the invariant locally closed subset $D^+\subset T^*N^+$. Every character nontrivial on $H$ is nonzero at the central Lie algebra element $(0,1)$. The restriction $$H^*_{T\times H}(D^+)\longrightarrow H^*_{T\times H}((D^+)^H)$$ is consequently an isomorphism there. Taking $\mathcal W$-invariants recovers $G\times H$ equivariance. This is compatible with localization over $\Lambda$: the point is $\mathcal W$-fixed, and the product of the Weyl translates of any denominator is an invariant denominator still nonzero there. This is also the central-parameter case of the prequotient localization in (Dumanski and Krylov 2025, Proposition 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-62"></a>[[DK25](#ref-DK25)].

<a id="eq:loop-higgs-localization"></a>
Both the added loops and their cotangent partners have nonzero $H$-weights, so $(D^+)^H$ sets all these coordinates to zero. The enlarged moment map then restricts to the original moment map. Stability restricts to the original stability as well: under a $G$-equivariant closed inclusion, the Hilbert–Mumford test of a point and of every one-parameter subgroup is unchanged. Equivalently, semi-invariants lift by reductivity, and orbit closures remain in the closed subvariety. Hence $$(D^+)^H=D,$$ with $H$ acting trivially on the right. Freeness of the stable actions now gives a coefficient-compatible isomorphism $$\label{eq:loop-higgs-localization}
 H_H^*(X^+)\otimes_{R_0[z]}\Lambda
    \simeq (H^*(X)\otimes_\mathbb C\mathbb C[z])\otimes_{R_0[z]}\Lambda.$$ The localization theorem used here does not require compactness of $D^+$ or $X^+$.

*Descent.* Let $I_B$ and $I_X$ be the kernels of the coefficient maps from $R_0$ to $B_0$ and $H^*(X)$, respectively. Equations <a href="#eq:enlarged-flavor-isomorphism" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:enlarged-flavor-isomorphism">[eq:enlarged-flavor-isomorphism]</a>, <a href="#eq:loop-coulomb-localization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:loop-coulomb-localization">[eq:loop-coulomb-localization]</a>, and <a href="#eq:loop-higgs-localization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:loop-higgs-localization">[eq:loop-higgs-localization]</a> identify the localized quotients over the same ring $\Lambda$. Therefore $I_B\Lambda=I_X\Lambda$. Setting $z=1$ uses the identity $$\Lambda/(z-1)\Lambda=(R_0)_{\mathfrak m_0}$$ and yields $(I_B)_{\mathfrak m_0}=(I_X)_{\mathfrak m_0}$. Both ideals are homogeneous. If $h\in I_B$ is homogeneous, there is $s\in R_0$ with $s(0)\ne0$ and $sh\in I_X$. The component of degree $\deg h$ of this relation is $s(0)h\in I_X$, whence $h\in I_X$. Interchanging the ideals proves $I_B=I_X$ globally. This is equality of ideals and gives the asserted graded isomorphism of quotient rings, with all nilpotents retained. The proof applies unchanged to empty stable loci; if there are no nonzero gauge ranks, the assertion is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-thm-full-torus"></a>
<div id="thm:full-torus" class="theorem">

**Theorem 24**. *For every datum in the statement of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, the coefficient map induces a canonical isomorphism of graded $R$-algebras $$B_F\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }H_F^*(X).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-full-torus"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-full-torus</summary>

*Proof.* At determinant stability, Proposition <a href="#prop:loop-removal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:loop-removal">23</a> identifies $B_0$ with ordinary cohomology, so $B_0$ is finite-dimensional. For any other regular stability, Lemma <a href="#lem:betti-independence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:betti-independence">21</a> gives the same total Betti number. The graded coefficient-compatible surjection $B_0\twoheadrightarrow H^*(X)$ of Proposition <a href="#prop:kernel-inclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:kernel-inclusion">8</a> is therefore an isomorphism. Lemma <a href="#lem:flavor-lift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:flavor-lift">22</a> proves the assertion for every commuting flavor torus, with the generality supplied by Lemma <a href="#lem:trivial-summands" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:trivial-summands">2</a>. If a quotient is empty, the same argument gives $B_0=B_F=0$. Uniqueness follows from surjectivity of the coefficient map $R\to B_F$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:cocharacters"></a>
### The cocharacter fixed scheme

We now compare the fixed scheme for the chosen cocharacter with the fixed scheme for the full topological torus. This comparison uses only minuscule generation, a root-support consequence of the loop-nilpotent Hall algebra, and the regularity of the stability. In particular, it does not use Theorem <a href="#thm:full-torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:full-torus">24</a> or assume coefficient surjectivity onto the cocharacter fixed quotient.

If $\nu\cdot\mathbf b\ne0$ for every $0<\mathbf b\leq\mathbf v$, Proposition <a href="#prop:minuscule" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:minuscule">3</a> immediately gives the comparison: every nontrivial positive or negative minuscule generator has nonzero $\nu$-weight. The argument below also covers regular characters that lie on some of these dimension hyperplanes.

For example, take two disconnected vertices, no arrows, and $\mathbf v=\mathbf w=(1,1)$, with $\nu=(1,-1)$. Each Higgs factor is a point with free stable gauge action, so this character is regular, although $\nu\cdot(1,1)=0$. The joint positive monopole is a product of monopoles of weights $1$ and $-1$, and hence vanishes in the cocharacter fixed quotient: weight zero alone does not imply survival.

To treat these characters, we adjoin a rank-one framing vertex. If a nontrivial minuscule generator of the original Coulomb algebra survived in its cocharacter fixed quotient, the corresponding class and its complementary class would both survive in the enlarged cocharacter fixed quotient. Polynomial Hall-algebra generation would then decompose both nonzero complementary dimension vectors into indecomposable dimension vectors of weight zero for the extended character. These two decompositions supply at least two blocks of a semistable direct sum; after removing the one ineffective diagonal scalar, its stabilizer remains positive-dimensional, contradicting regularity.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Generation in indecomposable charges

For a finite quiver $\Gamma$, possibly with loops, write $$\Delta_\Gamma
 =\{\,0\ne\boldsymbol\gamma\in\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}^{\Gamma_0}:
                  A_{\Gamma,\boldsymbol\gamma}(q)\ne0\,\}.$$ Here $A_{\Gamma,\boldsymbol\gamma}$ is the polynomial counting absolutely indecomposable representations over finite fields introduced in Kac’s work (Kac 1983) <a id="pm-cite-63"></a>[[Kac83](#ref-Kac83)]; polynomiality for quivers with self-loops is explicitly recalled in (Kac 2019, Theorem 1(a) and Remark 4) <a id="pm-cite-64"></a>[[Kac19](#ref-Kac19)]. We use $\Delta_\Gamma$ only as the nonzero support of these polynomials, without a loopless root-system interpretation.

The generation mechanism belongs to the cohomological Hall theory initiated by Kontsevich–Soibelman (Kontsevich and Soibelman 2011) <a id="pm-cite-65"></a>[[KontsevichSoibelman11](#ref-KontsevichSoibelman11)]. Its BPS and PBW structure was developed by Davison–Meinhardt and Davison (Davison and Meinhardt 2020; Davison 2025) <a id="pm-cite-66"></a>[[DavisonMeinhardt20](#ref-DavisonMeinhardt20), [Davison25AffineBPS](#ref-Davison25AffineBPS)]; the graded Kac identity builds on Mozgovoy’s generating-series formula and Davison’s cohomology of preprojective stacks (Mozgovoy 2011; Davison 2023) <a id="pm-cite-67"></a>[[Mozgovoy11McKay](#ref-Mozgovoy11McKay), [Davison23Preprojective](#ref-Davison23Preprojective)]. The difference-operator maps were developed in the work of Finkelberg–Tsymbaliuk, Frassek–Tsymbaliuk, and Tsymbaliuk (Finkelberg and Tsymbaliuk 2019; Frassek and Tsymbaliuk 2022; Tsymbaliuk 2023) <a id="pm-cite-68"></a>[[FinkelbergTsymbaliuk19Integral](#ref-FinkelbergTsymbaliuk19Integral), [FrassekTsymbaliuk22](#ref-FrassekTsymbaliuk22), [Tsymbaliuk23Difference](#ref-Tsymbaliuk23Difference)]. For arbitrary unframed quivers, Muthiah–Weekes relate Kac-polynomial Hilbert series and shuffle algebras through limit quantized zastava spaces (Muthiah and Weekes 2026) <a id="pm-cite-69"></a>[[MuthiahWeekes2026Zastavas](#ref-MuthiahWeekes2026Zastavas)]. The zastava comparison provides related context. Our inputs are the polynomial-parameter loop-nilpotent package of Jindal–Neguţ (Jindal and Neguţ 2026) <a id="pm-cite-70"></a>[[JindalNegut26](#ref-JindalNegut26)] and Tsymbaliuk’s arbitrary-quiver operator maps (Tsymbaliuk 2023, Theorem 5.7) <a id="pm-cite-71"></a>[[Tsymbaliuk23Difference](#ref-Tsymbaliuk23Difference)]. We first fix their product conventions and prove that the maps restrict to polynomial coefficient rings. This is needed before specializing the independent parameters to the given flavor action.

<a id="eq:hall-sign-product"></a>
Put $$S=\mathbb Q[\hbar,u_a\mid a\in\Gamma_1],
 \qquad K=\operatorname{Frac}(S),$$ with independent parameters. Let $\mathcal S_S^+$ be the polynomial shuffle half of (Jindal and Neguţ 2026, Definition 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-72"></a>[[JindalNegut26](#ref-JindalNegut26)], and denote its product by $*$. It is graded by dimension vectors, and contains $$e_{\mathbf n,g}
   =g(z_{ia})\prod_i\prod_{a,b=1}^{n_i}
                   (z_{ib}-z_{ia}+\hbar),
 \qquad
 g\in S[z_{ia}]^{\prod_iS_{n_i}}.$$ For homogeneous elements of dimensions $\mathbf n,\mathbf m$, use the product $$\label{eq:hall-sign-product}
 E\star E'
   =(-1)^{\sum_{a:i\to j}n_jm_i}E*E'.$$ Bilinearity of the exponent makes this product associative. Let $\mathcal S_S^-$ be its ordinary opposite algebra, with $f_{\mathbf n,g}$ denoting the same polynomial in that half. A word changes under this twist by a sign depending only on the dimensions of its factors; all homogeneous $S$-word spans are unchanged.

<a id="stmt-lem-polynomial-monopole-maps"></a>
<div id="lem:polynomial-monopole-maps" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Polynomial monopole maps). *For every unframed quiver $\Gamma$ and dimension vector $\mathbf d$, the two halves above admit $S$-linear algebra maps to an algebra of difference operators defined without inverting rotation or arrow parameters. They send $e_{\mathbf n,g}$ and $f_{\mathbf n,g}$, for $\mathbf n\leq\mathbf d$, to nonzero constant multiples of the positive and negative fundamental operators, respectively; they send these elements to zero when $\mathbf n\not\leq\mathbf d$. After setting $\hbar=0$, $w_{ia}=x_{ia}$, and $u_a=-f_a$, where $f_a$ is the prescribed flavor weight of arrow $a$, their images lie in $A_{\Gamma,\mathbf d,F}$. Dimension-vector degree becomes positive, respectively negative, topological charge. These maps preserve finite polynomial-coefficient identities under this specialization.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-polynomial-monopole-maps"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-polynomial-monopole-maps</summary>

*Proof.* We specify the operator algebra and the sign convention. Let $D_{ia}w_{jb}=(w_{jb}+\hbar\delta_{ij}\delta_{ab})D_{ia}$, with all the $D_{ia}$ commuting. Allow coefficients in $$S[w_{ia},(w_{ia}-w_{ib}+r\hbar)^{-1}:
                      a\ne b,\ r\in\mathbb Z].$$ This ring is preserved by every shift $D_{ia}^{\pm1}$. For subsets $A_i\subseteq\{1,\ldots,d_i\}$, write $w_A=(w_{ia})_{a\in A_i,i\in\Gamma_0}$. Define $$\begin{aligned}
 E_{\mathbf n,g}
 &=\sum_{|A_i|=n_i}
   g(w_A)
   \frac{\displaystyle\prod_{a:i\to j}
             \prod_{\alpha\in A_i,\ \beta\notin A_j}
                (w_{i\alpha}-w_{j\beta}+u_a)}
        {\displaystyle\prod_i
             \prod_{\alpha\in A_i,\ \beta\notin A_i}
                (w_{i\alpha}-w_{i\beta})}
       \prod_{i,\alpha\in A_i}D_{i\alpha},\\
 F_{\mathbf n,g}
 &=\sum_{|A_i|=n_i}
   g(w_A-\hbar)
   \frac{\displaystyle\prod_{a:j\to i}
             \prod_{\alpha\in A_i,\ \beta\notin A_j}
                (w_{i\alpha}-w_{j\beta}-u_a)}
        {\displaystyle\prod_i
             \prod_{\alpha\in A_i,\ \beta\notin A_i}
                (w_{i\alpha}-w_{i\beta})}
       \prod_{i,\alpha\in A_i}D_{i\alpha}^{-1}.
\end{aligned}$$ These are the fundamental formulas (Jindal and Neguţ 2026, Equations (78)–(79)) <a id="pm-cite-73"></a>[[JindalNegut26](#ref-JindalNegut26)]. Let $\mathcal C_S$ be the $S$-subalgebra generated by these operators and the polynomials symmetric in $w$ separately at each vertex.

We construct the maps first over $K$, then prove that their polynomial halves land in $\mathcal C_S$. Use Tsymbaliuk’s maps on the full trigonometric shuffle algebra and its opposite (Tsymbaliuk 2023, Theorem 5.7, Equations (93) and (96)) <a id="pm-cite-74"></a>[[Tsymbaliuk23Difference](#ref-Tsymbaliuk23Difference)], with no framing, and make the formal substitutions $$q=e^{\varepsilon\hbar},\qquad
 t_a=e^{\varepsilon u_a},\qquad
 W_{ia}=e^{\varepsilon w_{ia}}.$$ Here $\varepsilon$ is an auxiliary formal variable, not $\hbar$. The multiplicative shift in the source satisfies $D^{\mathrm{trig}}_{ia}W_{ia}=q^{-1}W_{ia}D^{\mathrm{trig}}_{ia}$. Thus its inverse becomes our positive shift $D_{ia}$, while it becomes $D_{ia}^{-1}$ in the negative map. The leading coefficient of the trigonometric shuffle kernel is $$\left(\frac{x-\hbar}{x}\right)^{\delta_{ij}}
 \prod_{a:i\to j}[-(x+u_a)]
 \prod_{a:j\to i}(x+\hbar-u_a).$$ It is precisely the kernel for $\star$ in Equation <a href="#eq:hall-sign-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hall-sign-product">[eq:hall-sign-product]</a>: the incoming-arrow signs have been removed from the kernel of (Jindal and Neguţ 2026, Equation (31)) <a id="pm-cite-75"></a>[[JindalNegut26](#ref-JindalNegut26)]. This distinction is necessary. For the quiver consisting of one arrow $i\to j$, the untwisted product gives $e_{(1,0),1}*e_{(0,1),1}
   =-e_{(1,1),z_i-z_j+u_a}$, whereas the corresponding fundamental operator product has a plus sign.

Here are the order calculations that justify taking leading coefficients, including for loops and repeated gauge slots. Put $$\kappa(\mathbf n)=\sum_{a:i\to j}n_in_j,\qquad
 \ell_i=\#\{a:i\to i\},\qquad
 L_i^+=\sum_{a:i\to j}d_j-d_i+1,\qquad
 L_i^-=\sum_{a:j\to i}d_j-d_i+1.$$ Lift a polynomial $E$ of dimension $\mathbf n$ to $\varepsilon^{\kappa(\mathbf n)}
 E((X_{ia}-1)/\varepsilon)$. The cross-kernel order is $\kappa(\mathbf n+\mathbf m)-\kappa(\mathbf n)-\kappa(\mathbf m)$; thus the leading source product is $\star$. In an operator summand, let $m_{ia}$ be the length of the string assigned to gauge slot $(i,a)$, so $\sum_a m_{ia}=n_i$, and set $P_2=\sum_{i,a}\ell_i m_{ia}^2$. The three prefactors in Tsymbaliuk’s formulas have orders $$\begin{array}{c|c}
 \text{factor}&\varepsilon\text{-order}\\ \hline
 \text{single-entry factors }Y\text{ or }Y'
     &L^\pm\cdot\mathbf n-\sum_i\ell_i n_i\\
 \text{inverse kernels within one string}
     &-P_2+\sum_i\ell_i n_i\\
 \text{factors between distinct slots}
     &-\kappa(\mathbf n)+P_2 .
 \end{array}$$ Together with the lifted input, their total order is $L^\pm\cdot\mathbf n$, independently of the composition. Multiply the operator image of the lift by $\varepsilon^{-L^\pm\cdot\mathbf n}$ and take its $\varepsilon^0$ coefficient to define the normalized leading map. The remaining denominators have nonzero leading coefficients over $K(w)$. There are only finitely many compositions. Consequently an input with zero normalized leading coefficient also has zero normalized leading output. The construction is independent of the lift. Since $L^\pm\cdot\mathbf n$ is additive, the normalized leading maps preserve multiplication. Multiplying them in dimension $\mathbf n$ by $\hbar^{-|\mathbf n|}$ preserves that property.

The use of formal parameters in this argument requires no analytic specialization theorem. For real $\hbar<u_a<0$ and small positive $\varepsilon$, the substitutions satisfy Tsymbaliuk’s parameter inequalities. The monomial factors involving logarithms of torus parameters are expanded near $W=1$; they have leading coefficient one and rational Taylor coefficients in $\hbar,u_a,w_{ia}$. Each coefficient of the multiplication identities is therefore a rational identity valid on this nonempty real open set, hence valid over $K(w)$. This justifies the formal leading maps, denoted $\Psi_+$ and $\Psi_-$.

<a id="eq:corrected-monopole-images"></a>
For the distinguished elements the maps are especially simple: $$\label{eq:corrected-monopole-images}
 \Psi_+(e_{\mathbf n,g})
   =(-1)^{L^+\cdot\mathbf n+\kappa(\mathbf n)}E_{\mathbf n,g},
 \qquad
 \Psi_-(f_{\mathbf n,g})=F_{\mathbf n,g}.$$ Indeed, the same-color factor in $e_{\mathbf n,g}$ vanishes on every additive string of length at least two. Only selected subsets of distinct slots remain. Its diagonal factors contribute $\hbar^{|\mathbf n|}$, which cancels the normalization $\hbar^{-|\mathbf n|}$; its off-diagonal factors cancel the same-color denominators in the operator formula. The factors for arrows between two selected slots cancel as well. In the positive formula the single-entry factors contribute $(-1)^{\sum_i n_i(L_i^+-\ell_i)}$, and the internal-arrow cancellations contribute $(-1)^{\kappa(\mathbf n)-\sum_i\ell_i n_i}$, giving the stated sign. In the opposite formula the selected variables are $w_A-\hbar$; the incoming-arrow factors cancel without these signs. This proves both formulas, and proves that the image is zero when $\mathbf n\not\leq\mathbf d$.

By (Jindal and Neguţ 2026, Theorem 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-76"></a>[[JindalNegut26](#ref-JindalNegut26)], the distinguished elements generate $\mathcal S_S^+$ over $S$. The twist preserves their word spans, and the opposite statement follows by reversing words. Equation <a href="#eq:corrected-monopole-images" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:corrected-monopole-images">[eq:corrected-monopole-images]</a> therefore proves that both polynomial halves land in $\mathcal C_S$. Although we constructed the maps over $K$, their restrictions are thus defined over $S$; no inverse of a parameter is needed in the identities in $\mathcal C_S$.

Finally specialize $\hbar=0$, $w_{ia}=x_{ia}$, and $u_a=-f_a$. Every allowed denominator becomes $x_{ia}-x_{ib}$, with $a\ne b$, so this defines a homomorphism to $\operatorname{Frac}(P_F)[X_*(T)]$, sending each $D_{ia}$ to the monomial for its standard slot coweight. For a selected-variable dressing $g$, also denoting its specialization by $g$, direct comparison with Proposition <a href="#prop:minuscule" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:minuscule">3</a> gives $$M^+_{\mathbf n,g}
   =(-1)^{\sum_{a:i\to j}n_i(d_j-n_j)}
          E_{\mathbf n,g}\big|_{\mathrm{specialization}},
 \qquad
 M^-_{\mathbf n,g}
   =(-1)^{\sum_i n_i(d_i-n_i)}
          F_{\mathbf n,g}\big|_{\mathrm{specialization}}.$$ The first sign comes from the outgoing matter factors; the second comes from reversing the minuscule tangent weights. The coefficient roots themselves are unchanged. Thus every generator of $\mathcal C_S$ specializes to an element of $A_{\Gamma,\mathbf d,F}$, and so does every image of the polynomial halves. Only this homomorphism is needed; no injectivity or flatness after specialization is asserted. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-root-charge-generation"></a>
<div id="lem:root-charge-generation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:root-charge-words"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Root-charge generation). *Let $\Gamma$ be an unframed quiver with dimension vector $\mathbf d$, and let a flavor torus act by a character on each arrow. Write $A_{\Gamma,\mathbf d,F}$ for its commutative Coulomb algebra and $R_{\Gamma,\mathbf d,F}$ for its coefficient algebra. For $\epsilon\in\{+1,-1\}$, let $M_{\mathbf b,p}^{\epsilon}$ be a dressed minuscule class whose coweight has $b_i$ entries equal to $\epsilon$ at vertex $i$ and all other entries zero, where $0<\mathbf b\leq\mathbf d$. There is a finite expression $$\label{eq:root-charge-words}
 M_{\mathbf b,p}^{\epsilon}
   =\sum_r c_r a_{r1}\cdots a_{rk_r},
 \qquad c_r\in R_{\Gamma,\mathbf d,F},$$ in which every factor is topologically homogeneous and $$\deg_{\mathrm{top}}a_{rh}=\epsilon\boldsymbol\gamma_{rh},
 \qquad
 \boldsymbol\gamma_{rh}\in\Delta_\Gamma,
 \qquad
 \sum_{h=1}^{k_r}\boldsymbol\gamma_{rh}=\mathbf b.$$ The factors need not themselves be minuscule classes.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-root-charge-generation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-root-charge-generation</summary>

*Proof.* *Polynomial Hall-algebra input.* Use the independent polynomial ring $S$ defined above. This is the equivariant coefficient ring $H^*(BT_{\mathrm{univ}};\mathbb Q)$ for $T_{\mathrm{univ}}=(\mathbb C^\times)^{1+|\Gamma_1|}$, with independent rotation and arrow characters. It lies in the geometric parameter setting of (Jindal and Neguţ 2026, Equation (30) and Remark 2.6) <a id="pm-cite-77"></a>[[JindalNegut26](#ref-JindalNegut26)]; no extension to arbitrary field-valued exponential parameters is needed. On the tripled quiver use weights $-u_a$, $u_a-\hbar$, and $\hbar$ on an original arrow, its reverse, and an added loop, respectively. These choices satisfy all the genericity hypotheses of (Jindal and Neguţ 2026, sec. 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-78"></a>[[JindalNegut26](#ref-JindalNegut26)]; in particular there is no nonzero nonnegative integral relation among $u_a,\hbar-u_a$, and $u_a\notin\mathbb Z\hbar$ for every original loop.

We use the following polynomial-form results of that source: the loop-nilpotent Hall algebra identifies with its shuffle half (Jindal and Neguţ 2026, Proposition 3.7) <a id="pm-cite-79"></a>[[JindalNegut26](#ref-JindalNegut26)]; its BPS descendants generate over $S$ (Jindal and Neguţ 2026, Theorem 3.10, Equation (109), and Corollary 3.15) <a id="pm-cite-80"></a>[[JindalNegut26](#ref-JindalNegut26)]; and the BPS comparison and Kac formula show that the nonzero dimension-vector support of these generators is $\Delta_\Gamma$ (Jindal and Neguţ 2026, Proposition 3.14 and §3.10) <a id="pm-cite-81"></a>[[JindalNegut26](#ref-JindalNegut26)]. Here the Kac identity retains the full cohomological grading: the coefficient of $t^{d/2}$ is $(-1)^d\dim H^d$. A zero Kac polynomial therefore forces every graded piece of the corresponding BPS space to vanish. Proposition 3.14 of that source transfers this vanishing to the loop-nilpotent BPS space. The concrete twist in Equation <a href="#eq:hall-sign-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hall-sign-product">[eq:hall-sign-product]</a> preserves these homogeneous word spans. Here polynomial form refers to equivariant parameters over $\mathbb Q$, not integral cohomology.

Apply the generation statement to $e_{\mathbf b,g}$ or $f_{\mathbf b,g}$, and retain dimension-vector degree $\mathbf b$. All nonconstant Hall degrees are nonnegative, so the result is a finite $S$-linear combination of products of BPS descendants with degrees in $\Delta_\Gamma$ summing to $\mathbf b$. Applying Lemma <a href="#lem:polynomial-monopole-maps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:polynomial-monopole-maps">25</a> gives the corresponding finite identity in $\mathcal C_S$. Its coefficients lie in $S$; no parameter from $S$ is inverted in this identity. Every factor has degree at most $\mathbf b\leq\mathbf d$; no assertion about vanishing of entire Hall components outside this bound is needed.

*Specialization.* Now extend scalars to $\mathbb C$ and apply the specialization homomorphism of Lemma <a href="#lem:polynomial-monopole-maps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:polynomial-monopole-maps">25</a>. Each finite identity becomes an identity in $A_{\Gamma,\mathbf d,F}$. The signs in the images of the distinguished elements are nonzero constants and can be absorbed in its coefficients. This operation is performed on the abstract polynomial-form algebra and its map, not on the ambient polynomial representation of the shuffle algebra. An element whose ambient representative is divisible by $\hbar$ need not belong to $\hbar$ times the polynomial shuffle half, and is therefore not discarded before applying the map.

*General dressings.* It remains to allow an arbitrary stabilizer-invariant dressing $p$. At each vertex, the polynomial algebra symmetric separately in the selected and complementary variables is generated by the polynomials symmetric in all variables together with those symmetric in the selected variables. Indeed, each complementary power sum is the total power sum minus the selected power sum. Thus $p$ is a finite sum of coefficient polynomials times selected-variable dressings $g$. The already proved identities for $g$ give Equation <a href="#eq:root-charge-words" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:root-charge-words">[eq:root-charge-words]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Indecomposables and central moment values

We record the geometric consequence of the charges in Lemma <a href="#lem:root-charge-generation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:root-charge-generation">26</a>. For a dimension vector $\boldsymbol\gamma$, put $$G_{\boldsymbol\gamma}=\prod_{i\in\Gamma_0}\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}_{\gamma_i},
 \qquad
 N_{\boldsymbol\gamma}=\bigoplus_{a:i\to j}
                     \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(\mathbb C^{\gamma_i},\mathbb C^{\gamma_j}).$$ For a real vector $\boldsymbol\theta=(\theta_i)$, let $\tau_{\boldsymbol\theta}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Lie}}(G_{\boldsymbol\gamma})^*$ be the central functional $$\tau_{\boldsymbol\theta}(\xi)
       =\sum_i\theta_i\operatorname{tr}(\xi_i).$$ Choose standard Hermitian forms, and write $(\mu_{\mathbb R},\mu_{\mathbb C})$ for the flat hyperkähler moment maps on $T^*N_{\boldsymbol\gamma}$. We normalize the real parameter so that an integral character $\boldsymbol\theta$ corresponds to real central level $\tau_{\boldsymbol\theta}$ in the affine Kempf–Ness correspondence. With any other common normalization, the same fixed scalar multiplies every level below.

<a id="stmt-lem-root-moment"></a>
<div id="lem:root-moment" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27**. *If $\boldsymbol\gamma\in\Delta_\Gamma$ and $\boldsymbol\theta\cdot\boldsymbol\gamma=0$, then $$\mu_{\mathbb C}^{-1}(0)\cap
 \mu_{\mathbb R}^{-1}(\tau_{\boldsymbol\theta})\ne\varnothing.$$ The scalar circle acting simultaneously on all nonzero vertex spaces fixes every point of this representation.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-root-moment"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-root-moment</summary>

*Proof.* First, $\Gamma$ has an indecomposable complex representation $x$ of dimension $\boldsymbol\gamma$. For completeness, the required passage from Kac support to characteristic zero can be made by constructibility. A representation is decomposable over an algebraic closure exactly when its endomorphism algebra contains an idempotent other than $0$ and $1$. The equations for such idempotents, together with these two open exclusions, define a constructible incidence condition over $\mathbb Z$. Chevalley’s theorem therefore gives a constructible locus of geometrically indecomposable representations. A nonzero Kac polynomial gives points of this locus in infinitely many residue characteristics. Its generic fiber cannot be empty, since a constructible subset of $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\mathbb Z$ that omits the generic point is finite. Base change of the nonempty characteristic-zero locus to $\mathbb C$ gives $x$.

The following trace-annihilator argument is the lifting criterion of Crawley–Boevey (Crawley-Boevey 2001, Lemma 3.1 and Theorem 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-82"></a>[[CrawleyBoevey01](#ref-CrawleyBoevey01)]; we recall it before passing to the real moment level. Every endomorphism $e$ of $x$ has the form $c\mathrm{id}+n$, where $n$ is nilpotent. Indeed, its generalized eigenspaces are subrepresentations, so indecomposability permits only one eigenvalue. Consequently $$\tau_{\boldsymbol\theta}(e)
 =c\,\boldsymbol\theta\cdot\boldsymbol\gamma=0.$$ The cotangent moment map with $x$ fixed is the transpose of the infinitesimal orbit map: $$N_{\boldsymbol\gamma}^*\longrightarrow
       \mathop{\mathrm{Lie}}(G_{\boldsymbol\gamma})^*,
 \qquad
 y\longmapsto\bigl(\xi\longmapsto y(\xi x)\bigr).$$ Its image is the annihilator of $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_\Gamma(x)=\ker(\xi\mapsto\xi x)$. Hence the complex moment fiber at $\tau_{\boldsymbol\theta}$ is nonempty, as is the fiber at any common scalar multiple of that value.

This fiber is a closed invariant affine subset, since the moment value is central. The closure of any orbit in it contains an orbit closed in the ambient affine representation. By the zero-character affine Kempf–Ness theorem, that orbit meets $\mu_{\mathbb R}^{-1}(0)$; see (King 1994, Theorem 6.1 and Corollary 6.2) <a id="pm-cite-83"></a>[[King94](#ref-King94)]. Thus there is a point with hyperkähler moment value $(0,\tau_{\boldsymbol\theta},0)$, using the real and imaginary parts of the complex moment map and adjusting the common scalar to the chosen normalization.

The quaternionic rotation on the flat cotangent representation commutes with the compact gauge group and rotates its three moment maps. A single rotation sends this value to $(\tau_{\boldsymbol\theta},0,0)$ (Hitchin et al. 1987, sec. 3(D)) <a id="pm-cite-84"></a>[[HKLR87](#ref-HKLR87)]. This proves the asserted nonemptiness. The simultaneous scalar acts trivially on all arrow spaces and their duals, proving the last statement. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### The framing vertex and complementary monopoles

<a id="stmt-prop-cocharacter-fixed"></a>
<div id="prop:cocharacter-fixed" class="proposition">

**Proposition 28**. *Suppose that every $\nu$-semistable point of $\mu^{-1}(0)$ is stable and has trivial $G$-stabilizer. Then the natural quotient map $$A_F/\langle (A_F)_m:m\ne0\rangle
       \longrightarrow B_F,$$ where $m$ denotes $\nu$-weight, is an isomorphism. This is an equality of quotients of $A_F$, preserving the coefficient map and both gradings.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-cocharacter-fixed"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-cocharacter-fixed</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#lem:trivial-summands" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:trivial-summands">2</a> allows us to suppose that $F$ scales the individual arrows and framing rows. Adding or removing the gauge-trivial cotangent summands in that lemma preserves both regularity and all the fixed quotients under consideration. Write $$\nu((g_i))=\prod_i\det(g_i)^{\nu_i}.$$ Adjoin one vertex $\infty$ of dimension one and $w_i$ arrows $i\to\infty$, keeping the original arrows. Denote this unframed quiver by $\widehat Q$ and its dimension vector by $\mathbf d=(\mathbf v,1)$. Its representation is the original $N$, after a choice of basis in each framing space.

<a id="eq:central-splitting"></a>
There is a group isomorphism $$G\times\mathbb C^\times\longrightarrow G_{\mathbf d},
 \qquad ((g_i),c)\longmapsto((cg_i),c).$$ The second factor acts trivially on $N$. The BFN space of triples and its convolution therefore split, giving $$\label{eq:central-splitting}
 A_{\widehat Q,\mathbf d,F}
       \cong A_F\otimes_\mathbb C\mathbb C[\xi,z,z^{-1}].$$ Here $\xi$ is the coefficient parameter of the extra scalar and $z$ is its fundamental Laurent monopole, of cohomological degree zero. This $z$ is unrelated to the degree-two added-loop mass in Section <a href="#sec:deformations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:deformations">7</a>. In the original vertex coordinates, $z$ has topological charge $\mathbf d$: it translates every gauge coweight entry, including the entry at $\infty$, by one.

Extend the character by $$\widehat\nu_i=\nu_i\quad(i\in Q_0),
 \qquad
 \widehat\nu_\infty=-\sum_i\nu_i v_i.$$ Then $\widehat\nu\cdot\mathbf d=0$, and under the displayed group isomorphism $\widehat\nu$ is $\nu$ on $G$ and trivial on the extra scalar. In particular, both $\xi$ and $z^{\pm1}$ have $\widehat\nu$-weight zero. Taking the cocharacter fixed quotient in Equation <a href="#eq:central-splitting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:central-splitting">[eq:central-splitting]</a> consequently gives $$\bigl(A_{\widehat Q,\mathbf d,F}\bigr)_{\mathrm{fix},\widehat\nu}
 \cong
 \bigl(A_F\bigr)_{\mathrm{fix},\nu}
                 \otimes_\mathbb C\mathbb C[\xi,z,z^{-1}],$$ where the subscript denotes the quotient by nonzero cocharacter weights. This extension is faithfully flat.

<a id="eq:complementary-monopoles"></a>
Let $M_{\mathbf b',p}^{+}$ be a nontrivial positive minuscule generator of $A_F$, and put $\mathbf b=(\mathbf b',0)$. If $\widehat x_{ia}$ denotes an enlarged gauge Chern root, the group isomorphism above identifies the original root with $x_{ia}=\widehat x_{ia}-\xi$. Let $p^{\uparrow}$ be the polynomial obtained from $p$ by these substitutions, leaving every flavor parameter unchanged. The original generator is then the positive minuscule class $M_{\mathbf b,p^{\uparrow}}^{+}$. Central translation also expresses it using the complementary negative coweight: $$\label{eq:complementary-monopoles}
 M_{\mathbf b,p^{\uparrow}}^{+}
       =z\,M_{\mathbf d-\mathbf b,p^{\uparrow}}^{-}.$$ The two coweights have the same Weyl stabilizer, so the same polynomial $p^{\uparrow}$ is an admissible dressing on both sides. It need not depend only on the variables selected by the complementary negative coweight; Lemma <a href="#lem:root-charge-generation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:root-charge-generation">26</a> allows all stabilizer-invariant dressings. The equality follows directly from Equation <a href="#eq:minuscule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:minuscule">[eq:minuscule]</a>: all matter weights pair trivially with the central coweight, and central translation identifies the minuscule orbits and their equivariant tangent bundles. Equivalently it follows from convolution with the invertible central class. Thus it holds with compatible fundamental-class conventions; rescaling minuscule generators changes only a nonzero constant.

Suppose that this generator has nonzero image in the cocharacter fixed quotient. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:root-charge-generation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:root-charge-generation">26</a> in the enlarged quiver to $M_{\mathbf b,p^{\uparrow}}^{+}$. Any word containing a factor of nonzero $\widehat\nu$-weight vanishes in that quotient. A nonzero image therefore requires a decomposition $$\mathbf b=\sum_a\boldsymbol\alpha_a,
 \qquad
 \boldsymbol\alpha_a\in\Delta_{\widehat Q},
 \qquad
 \widehat\nu\cdot\boldsymbol\alpha_a=0.$$ Since $z$ is an invertible element of weight zero, Equation <a href="#eq:complementary-monopoles" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:complementary-monopoles">[eq:complementary-monopoles]</a> implies that $M_{\mathbf d-\mathbf b,p^{\uparrow}}^{-}$ also survives. Applying the negative half of the same lemma yields $$\mathbf d-\mathbf b=\sum_b\boldsymbol\beta_b,
 \qquad
 \boldsymbol\beta_b\in\Delta_{\widehat Q},
 \qquad
 \widehat\nu\cdot\boldsymbol\beta_b=0.$$ Both sums are nonempty: $\mathbf b\ne0$, while its $\infty$-entry is zero and that of $\mathbf d$ is one.

Together these decompositions express $\mathbf d$ as a sum of at least two nonzero dimension vectors $\boldsymbol\gamma_1,\ldots,\boldsymbol\gamma_k$ in $\Delta_{\widehat Q}$, each orthogonal to $\widehat\nu$. For each summand choose the point furnished by Lemma <a href="#lem:root-moment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:root-moment">27</a>, with real central parameter $\widehat\nu$. Use orthogonal decompositions of the vertex spaces and take the direct sum of these points. Moment maps are block diagonal on direct sums, so the resulting point has $$\mu_{\mathbb C}=0,\qquad
 \mu_{\mathbb R}=\tau_{\widehat\nu}.$$ Its stabilizer contains $k$ independent block scalar circles.

The enlarged reduction is exactly the original framed reduction: the extra diagonal scalar is ineffective, its moment equation is redundant, and $\widehat\nu$ is trivial on it. After dividing by this scalar, the stabilizer still contains a torus of dimension $k-1>0$. By the affine Kempf–Ness correspondence at the character level, the resulting point is $\nu$-semistable in the original zero complex moment fiber (King 1994, Theorem 6.1 and Corollary 6.2) <a id="pm-cite-85"></a>[[King94](#ref-King94)]. This contradicts the assumed freeness of every such point. The argument also applies when the stable locus is empty: it would then produce a semistable point where none exists.

Every nontrivial positive minuscule generator therefore vanishes. For a negative generator use the identity $M_{\mathbf b,p^{\uparrow}}^{-}
  =z^{-1}M_{\mathbf d-\mathbf b,p^{\uparrow}}^{+}$ and interchange the two halves in the argument; it vanishes as well. By Proposition <a href="#prop:minuscule" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:minuscule">3</a>, the ideal they generate is the ideal generated by the nonzero full topological-charge spaces. It is therefore contained in the ideal generated by the nonzero $\nu$-weight spaces. For $m\ne0$, the $\nu$-weight space $(A_F)_m$ is a sum of nonzero topological-charge spaces. This gives the opposite inclusion. The ideals coincide, proving the proposition. If all gauge ranks are zero there are no such generators and the same conclusion is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Completion of the proof

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-cocharacter-fixed-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-cocharacter-fixed</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Proposition <a href="#prop:cocharacter-fixed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:cocharacter-fixed">28</a> and Theorem <a href="#thm:full-torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:full-torus">24</a> give graded $R$-algebra isomorphisms $$\mathbb C[Y_{\mathfrak f}^{\nu}]
   \xrightarrow[\ \sim\ ]{\beta} B_F
   \xrightarrow[\ \sim\ ]{\alpha} H_F^*(X),
 \qquad (\alpha\circ\beta)\circ\phi_C=\phi_H.$$ Thus $\ker\phi_C=\ker\phi_H$. The inverse of $\alpha\circ\beta$ is the isomorphism in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>; surjectivity of $\phi_H$ makes it the unique isomorphism compatible with the coefficient maps. All quotient rings are scheme-theoretic and retain their nilpotents. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The coefficient-ideal comparison $⇓$ $⇓$ $⇓$ The fixed-scheme comparison $⇓$ $⇓$ $⇓$ $\\displaystyle \\mathbb C[Y_{\\mathfrak f}^{\\nu}]\\ \\xrightarrow{\\ \\sim\\ }\\ B_F\\ \\xrightarrow{\\ \\sim\\ }\\ H_F^*(X)$ The two comparisons in the proof. Here $Q^{+}$ adds one loop at each positive-rank vertex of $Q$; dimension and framing vectors are unchanged and suppressed. The left column uses the stable-pair surjection in the dimension comparisons. The right column is independent of the left and identifies defining ideals, not just closed fixed points. Both final maps preserve the coefficient ring and grading.",
      "figure_id": "fig:proof-route",
      "pdf_page": 5,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:proof-route",
          "exact_quote": "The two comparisons in the proof.  Here $Q^+$ adds one loop at\neach positive-rank vertex of $Q$; dimension and framing vectors are\nunchanged and suppressed.  The left column uses the stable-pair\nsurjection in the dimension comparisons.  The",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "other",
      "tikz_pgf_source": null,
      "witness_description": "A flow diagram consisting of two parallel vertical columns of rectangular boxes connected by downward double arrows ($\\Downarrow$), followed by a horizontal sequence of arrows at the bottom.\n\nLeft column:\n- Column header: \"The coefficient-ideal comparison\"\n- Box 1:\n  \"Every $Q$, $F$ and regular $\\nu$\n  Stable-pair test\n  $I_{\\mathrm{top}} \\subseteq I_H$\n  Sections 3–4\"\n- Downward double arrow ($\\Downarrow$) to Box 2\n- Box 2:\n  \"$Q^+$, no flavor, determinant stability\n  Laurent model and cut-ideal count\n  $B_0(Q^+) \\simeq H^*(X_{\\mathrm{det}}(Q^+))$\n  Sections 5–6\"\n- Downward double arrow ($\\Downarrow$) to Box 3\n- Box 3:\n  \"Lift to the added-loop flavor $H$\n  Compare near gauge 0, mass $z = 1$\n  Descend homogeneous ideals for $Q$\n  Proposition 7.3\"\n- Downward double arrow ($\\Downarrow$) to Box 4\n- Box 4:\n  \"All regular stabilities, then all flavors\n  $B_F \\simeq H^*_F(X)$\n  Theorem 7.4\"\n\nRight column:\n- Column header: \"The fixed-scheme comparison\"\n- Box 1:\n  \"Suppose a nontrivial minuscule class\n  survives in the cocharacter quotient.\n  Adjoin a rank-one framing vertex;\n  its complementary class also survives\"\n- Downward double arrow ($\\Downarrow$) to Box 2\n- Box 2:\n  \"Polynomial Hall generation\n  Both surviving charges decompose\n  into indecomposable charges of the\n  enlarged quiver, all of weight zero\n  Lemma 8.2\"\n- Downward double arrow ($\\Downarrow$) to Box 3\n- Box 3:\n  \"Lemma 8.3 gives moment-level blocks.\n  Their direct sum retains a\n  positive-dimensional stabilizer after\n  removing the diagonal scalar,\n  contradicting regularity\"\n- Downward double arrow ($\\Downarrow$) to Box 4\n- Box 4:\n  \"$\\mathbb{C}[Y_f^\\nu] \\simeq B_F$\n  as quotients of $A_F$\n  Proposition 8.4\"\n\nBottom diagram centered below both columns:\n$\\mathbb{C}[Y_f^\\nu] \\xrightarrow{\\sim} B_F \\xrightarrow{\\sim} H^*_F(X)$"
    }
  ],
